## Replicability

necessary tools, procedures and capacities to investigate and monitor GHG emissions from their operations.

Fiduciary arrangements between governments and financial institutions as well as international organizations can be used to ensure the sustainability of these investments and carbon credits. These arrangements can also be used to facilitate the development of carbon markets and carbon trading systems in developing countries.

At present, clean fuel energy technologies are being developed and implemented in various countries around the world. In particular, carbon capture and storage (CCS) is being used to reduce emissions from power plants. This technology is becoming increasingly important as it can be used to reduce emissions from a wide range of industrial processes. Additionally, CCS can be used to reduce emissions from transportation systems.

If additional investments are made in these technologies, they will become more affordable and accessible to developing countries. This will allow them to implement the necessary measures to reduce their GHG emissions. Furthermore, with the testing of new technologies, it is possible that new methods for reducing GHG emissions will be developed. These methods could include the use of renewable energy sources or the development of new carbon capture and storage systems.

## Scalability

will work towards providing early-stage financing to develop innovative environmental solutions that can make a significant impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation. The programme will also conduct research with local communities to identify potential areas for investment in order to maximize the impact of the programme. The programme will be conducted through historical precedent and best practices.

The programme will focus on the following demonstration projects:

- A project to develop a new carbon capture and storage system.
- A project to develop a new renewable energy source.
- A project to develop a new method for reducing GHG emissions from transportation systems.
- A project to develop a new method for reducing GHG emissions from industrial processes.

The programme will be conducted with the aim of demonstrating the feasibility of these projects. The programme will also provide funding for the development and implementation of these projects. The programme will be conducted in partnership with local communities, governments and international organizations. The programme will also provide training and capacity building to ensure that the projects are successful.

### Challenges

The program's approach and financial structure may be perceived as an impediment to the widespread adoption of innovative technologies.

### Customers suitable for the program range from many consumer groups such as industrial, large commercial and small business customers who are interested in participating. Although these customers do not represent a significant portion of the overall market, they are often early adopters who drive the bulk of a regional market. This can be achieved by developing a long-term market strategy that increases the likelihood of large customers increasing their participation over time.

### Local Community Banks

Local community banks currently provide financing for small-scale renewable energy solutions to local businesses and households. These banks have the capacity to develop larger scale projects, such as solar farms or wind turbines, but lack the resources to do so. The program can help these banks by providing them with access to low-cost capital that they can use to finance these types of projects.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 84

## Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency

Distributed renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies are key participating instruments in the implementation of the [NDC](https://unfccc.int/process-and-mechanism/built-up/ndc-nationally-determined-contributions) and the achievement of sustainable development goals. The lack of standardized approaches to these technologies, inadequate financing, irregularities in the supply chain, limited capacity of the local workforce, and high costs for the same technology are some challenges that have hindered the deployment of renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies.

### Objective

- **Goal**: Accelerate & Deepen SCF
  - **Sub-Goal**: Enhance Technology
    - **Objective**: Promote Deployment of Renewable Energy Technologies

## Market Development

All the participating technology instruments (A1, A2) are market development instruments. These include measures about technology deployment, knowledge transfer, and capacity building. TAR is reduced, and the cost of renewable energy technologies is decreased. There is an increase in the use of renewable energy technologies for household clean cooking, solar water heating, and small-scale renewable energy services (including micro-grid and mini-grid). The cost of renewable energy technologies is reduced by 10-25%.

### Objective

- **Goal**: Accelerate & Deepen SCF
  - **Sub-Goal**: Enhance Technology
    - **Objective**: Promote Deployment of Renewable Energy Technologies

## Multilateral

Performance under the [SBI](https://unfccc.int/process-and-mechanism/built-up/sbi-special-climate-change-fund) programme and also under the [GCF](https://unfccc.int/process-and-mechanism/built-up/gcf-green-climate-fund) is enhanced. The performance of the renewable energy technologies is improved by 10-25%. The cost of renewable energy technologies is reduced by 10-25%.

### Objective

- **Goal**: Accelerate & Deepen SCF
  - **Sub-Goal**: Enhance Technology
    - **Objective**: Promote Deployment of Renewable Energy Technologies

## Multi-lateral

Performance under the [SBI](https://unfccc.int/process-and-mechanism/built-up/sbi-special-climate-change-fund) programme and also under the [GCF](https://unfccc.int/process-and-mechanism/built-up/gcf-green-climate-fund) is enhanced. The performance of the renewable energy technologies is improved by 10-25%. The cost of renewable energy technologies is reduced by 10-25%.

### Objective

- **Goal**: Accelerate & Deepen SCF
  - **Sub-Goal**: Enhance Technology
    - **Objective**: Promote Deployment of Renewable Energy Technologies

## Enhancing countries' capacities to mitigate greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in developing countries

### Participating countries are among the most vulnerable to climate change and have significant potential for reducing emissions through:

- **Afforestation and reforestation**: Planting new forests or restoring lost ones.
- **Avoided deforestation**: Preventing the loss of existing forests.

### Key elements of the model include:

1. **Participating countries** are among the most vulnerable to climate change and have significant potential for reducing emissions through:
   - Afforestation and reforestation
   - Avoided deforestation

2. **Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD)**:
   - Enhancing forest management
   - Promoting sustainable use of forests
   - Conserving biodiversity

3. **Technology transfer**: Providing access to clean technologies for sustainable forestry.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 8

## The LIF model is a key component of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) proposal in the context of climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### Core objectives & outcomes:
- **Objective:** To support developing countries to achieve their climate change mitigation and adaptation goals.
- **Outcome:** Enhanced capacity of developing countries to implement low-carbon, climate-resilient development pathways.

## Investment through the LIF component and across the GCF portfolio:

### Supporting activities from the LIF component:
- **Mainstreaming:** Mainstreaming climate change into national policies, strategies, and actions.
- **Capacity building:** Building capacities at the national level to implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.
- **Knowledge sharing:** Sharing knowledge and good practices among countries.

### Partnerships:
- **Partnerships:** Partnerships will engage with a wide range of stakeholders including governments, private sector entities, civil society organizations, and other relevant actors.
- **Collaboration:** Collaboration between the LIF component and other GCF-funded activities to enhance synergies and maximize impact.

## The largest portfolio of climate finance in the world:
- **Funding:** Providing funding for low-carbon, climate-resilient development projects.
- **Support:** Supporting developing countries in their efforts to mitigate and adapt to climate change.
- **Implementation:** Implementing projects that contribute to global climate goals while promoting sustainable development.

### Multi-country:
- **Objective:** To support multiple countries in achieving their climate change mitigation and adaptation objectives.
- **Funding:** Providing funding for projects that are implemented across multiple countries.
- **Coordination:** Coordinating efforts among countries to enhance synergies and maximize impact.
- **Capacity building:** Building capacities at the regional level to support implementation of climate change actions.

## E

At least three TSP-Assisted Countries (C) per cluster are planned in P.1, with an additional two countries planned for coverage in later phases such as CDB technical assistance design, standards, and capacity building. This will include the following: E&S, IFC, MAF, etc.

It is estimated that around 20 TSPs will be implemented across the three clusters. A total of at least 60 TSPs across the three clusters are then expected to align the sustainable development goals (SDGs) and are then expected to align the sustainable development goals (SDGs).

A total of four virtual exchange workshops will be organized by the GCF Secretariat, focusing on program benefits, learning, and best practices.

There are 20 member states and associate members of the CARICOM. It is estimated that around 15 countries will participate in the regional exchange workshop per CARICOM cluster.

There are also possibilities of organizing one regional exchange workshop with participation from other CDB's or other regional clusters.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v1.0 | PAGE 8

## E.E. Prospect/programme activities and deliverables

### Introduction

A prospect/programme should be based on a description of basic activities. Significant deliverables should be mentioned in these descriptions. Activities should be described in sufficient detail to allow for an assessment of the relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, sustainability, and potential impact of each activity. This section should also include a description of how the activities will be monitored and evaluated.

### Activities

| Activity | Description | Sub-Activities | Deliverables |
|----------|-------------|----------------|--------------|
| **Activity 1.1.1** | Improve regulatory frameworks for climate change adaptation and mitigation, including the implementation of the US Climate Change Act and associated regulations. | - 1.1.1.1 Review and regularly update existing legislation and regulations to ensure they are consistent with the objectives of the US Climate Change Act. <br> - 1.1.1.2 Assess the effectiveness of existing climate change adaptation and mitigation policies in achieving their intended outcomes. <br> - 1.1.1.3 Develop and implement new regulatory frameworks for climate change adaptation and mitigation. | - 1.1.1.1 Note on the regularity of updates to legislation and regulations. <br> - 1.1.1.2 Legal and regulatory amendments prepared. <br> - 1.1.1.3 Regulatory framework updated and submitted for approval. |
| **Activity 1.1.2** | Support the development and implementation of climate change adaptation strategies in key sectors. | - 1.1.2.1 Assess the vulnerability of key sectors to climate change impacts. <br> - 1.1.2.2 Develop and implement sector-specific adaptation plans. <br> - 1.1.2.3 Provide technical assistance to support the implementation of adaptation strategies in key sectors. | - 1.1.2.1 Sector vulnerability assessments completed. <br> - 1.1.2.2 Adaptation plans developed for each sector. <br> - 1.1.2.3 Technical assistance provided to implement adaptation strategies. |
| **Activity 1.1.3** | Enhance the capacity of national and local governments to integrate climate change adaptation into their policies, programmes, and budgets. | - 1.1.3.1 Assess the current level of integration of climate change adaptation into national and local government policies, programmes, and budgets. <br> - 1.1.3.2 Develop and implement training programmes for national and local government officials on integrating climate change adaptation into their policies, programmes, and budgets. | - 1.1.3.1 Assessment report submitted to the GCF. <br> - 1.1.3.2 Training programmes developed and implemented. |
| **Activity 1.1.4** | Support the development of climate-resilient infrastructure in key sectors. | - 1.1.4.1 Assess the vulnerability of key infrastructure to climate change impacts. <br> - 1.1.4.2 Develop and implement guidelines for climate-resilient infrastructure design and construction. <br> - 1.1.4.3 Provide technical assistance to support the implementation of climate-resilient infrastructure projects in key sectors. | - 1.1.4.1 Vulnerability assessments completed. <br> - 1.1.4.2 Guidelines for climate-resilient infrastructure developed and disseminated. <br> - 1.1.4.3 Technical assistance provided to implement climate-resilient infrastructure projects. |
| **Activity 1.1.5** | Promote public awareness and engagement on climate change adaptation and mitigation. | - 1.1.5.1 Develop and implement a national communication strategy on climate change adaptation and mitigation. <br> - 1.1.5.2 Conduct public awareness campaigns on climate change adaptation and mitigation. <br> - 1.1.5.3 Establish partnerships with civil society organizations to support public engagement on climate change adaptation and mitigation. | - 1.1.5.1 Communication strategy developed and implemented. <br> - 1.1.5.2 Public awareness campaigns conducted. <br> - 1.1.5.3 Partnerships established with civil society organizations. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 9

## Activity 1.1.2

Strengthen and operationalize the **National Adaptation Plan (NAP)** process to enhance climate change adaptation planning and implementation for sustainable development in line with the Paris Agreement.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.1

Address driver capacity frameworks, including gender-responsive climate-smart agriculture, for women and other marginalized groups.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.2

Enhance the integration of climate change adaptation into national development planning processes to better support sustainable development objectives and improve resilience to climate risks.

## Activity 1.1.3

Conduct capacity strengthening and awareness-raising in order to enhance knowledge, understanding, and application of the NAP process among relevant stakeholders.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.1

Enhance understanding of the NAP process among national and sub-national government officials, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders through targeted training and outreach activities.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.2

Regular programs to be submitted to the CDB for approval and funding, with a maximum of one submission per fiscal year.

## Raise awareness of RE/EE opportunities and potential savings

### Activity 1.2.1
**Objective:** Increase awareness of RE/EE opportunities and potential savings.

**Objective:** Develop a communication strategy for organizations.
- Design a comprehensive communication plan for various stakeholders.
- Ensure consistent messaging across all marketing channels.

- Create specific marketing materials aimed at different customer segments.
- Utilize local media to maximize reach and impact.

**Objective:** Develop a communication strategy for organizations.

## Activity 1.2.2: Provide technical assistance to support delivery of energy efficiency (EE) projects

### Sub-activity 1.2.2.1: Conduct a comprehensive assessment and provide recommendations on EE opportunities for the private sector
- **Objective:** To identify and assess EE opportunities in the private sector.
- **Outputs:**
  - Comprehensive assessment report.
  - Recommendations for EE improvements.

### Sub-activity 1.2.2.2: Setup technical assistance support to the private sector
- **Objective:** To provide ongoing technical support to the private sector.
- **Outputs:**
  - Technical assistance plan.
  - Regular progress reports.

## Activity 1.3.3: Develop and implement a monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) system

### Sub-activity 1.3.3.1: Conduct a feasibility study for an MRV system
- **Objective:** To assess the feasibility of implementing an MRV system.
- **Outputs:**
  - Feasibility study report.

### Sub-activity 1.3.3.2: Develop an MRV system
- **Objective:** To design and develop an MRV system for tracking EE progress.
- **Outputs:**
  - Detailed MRV system design.
  - Implementation plan.

### Sub-activity 1.3.3.3: Implement the MRV system
- **Objective:** To put in place the MRV system for monitoring EE activities.
- **Outputs:**
  - Operational MRV system.
  - Initial data collection and reporting.

## Activity 1.2.3: Provide technical assistance to support energy audits

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.1: Conduct energy audits
- **Objective:** To identify energy savings opportunities through audits.
- **Outputs:**
  - Energy audit reports.
  - Recommendations for energy efficiency improvements.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.2: Develop and implement a training program on energy auditing
- **Objective:** To build capacity in energy auditing within the country.
- **Outputs:**
  - Training curriculum.
  - Conducted training sessions.

## Activity 1.3.4: Develop and implement a financing mechanism

### Sub-activity 1.3.4.1: Identify potential financing sources
- **Objective:** To explore various funding options for EE projects.
- **Outputs:**
  - List of potential financiers.
  - Funding proposal outlines.

## E.7.2 Additional Information about Monitoring and Reporting Requirements

Additional information about monitoring and reporting requirements is included in Annex 21: Programme Guidelines Manual. ## E.7.3 Monitoring

Monitoring will be led by the CDB TA Executive Team, which will be overseen regularly by providing and contracting senior consultants with expertise in climate change mitigation and adaptation. The team will develop a plan and effective manner and set greater time and resource management for the implementation of the monitoring activities. The monitoring activities will include:

- Monitoring of the environmental and social risk management requirements.

Reporting from CDB to GCF: Using the data contained in the reporting provided by the National Executing Entities and the CDB FA Evaluation Team, as well as the data collected at the national level, the CDB Accredited Entity Team will prepare the GCF Reports with the following information:

- A mid-term evaluation (interim evaluation) report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the programs, which will be prepared by an independent external evaluator.
- A final evaluation report at the end of its implementation period of the program, which is expected to be prepared by the same independent external evaluator.

### E/F Evaluation

In the event that requirements, CDB will contract an external evaluator to conduct independent evaluations. These are the programme implementation period (mid-term) and final evaluation reports. The mid-term evaluation report will be based on a desk review of the quality of the data submitted by the National Executing Entities and the GCF Accredited Entity Team. The end term will be based on an external evaluator's assessment of the quality of the data submitted by the National Executing Entities and the GCF Accredited Entity Team.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 147

## E. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### E.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (Page 3 pages)

#### Selected Risk Factor 1 - Climate Change
**Category:** Development
**Probability:** Low
**Impact:** High

**Description:**
There is a moderate likelihood that utilities may encounter challenges in obtaining financing for climate change adaptation projects due to the lack of a robust regulatory framework. This could lead to delays and increased costs.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- Develop a comprehensive risk management strategy.
- Establish a dedicated team to address climate change risks.
- Implement regular risk assessments and updates.

#### Selected Risk Factor 2 - Capacity constraints among TSPs
**Category:** Technical and operational
**Probability:** Low
**Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**
There is a low likelihood that the capacity of technology service providers (TSPs) in the region will be insufficient to meet the high demand for RE & EE technologies.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- Enhance the capacity of TSPs through training and development.
- Encourage partnerships with international organizations.
- Promote public-private partnerships to share best practices.

#### Selected Risk Factor 3 - Regulatory and policy barriers
**Category:** Development
**Probability:** Moderate
**Impact:** High

**Description:**
There are significant regulatory frameworks and policies across the Caribbean that impede the implementation of RE & EE technologies.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- Conduct a comprehensive review of existing regulations.
- Engage with policymakers to identify barriers.
- Develop and implement policy reforms to facilitate RE & EE adoption.

### 7.1.1
Based on the experience of sub-entities as other implementers, it is likely that there will be additional challenges and risks associated with the use of sub-entities. This is partly because the use of the sub-entity model has been used in a limited number of cases. The use of sub-entities by the GCF is also likely to be different from the use of sub-entities by other climate change implementers. It is also likely that the use of sub-entities will be more complex and require additional resources, particularly if the use of an entity such as a trust or a foundation is considered.

### 7.1.2
To mitigate the use of higher-risk arrangements, the Programme has identified several measures to ensure that the risks are managed effectively. These include: (i) ensuring that the sub-entities have sufficient capacity and expertise; (ii) ensuring that the sub-entities have adequate financial resources; (iii) ensuring that the sub-entities have adequate financial resources; # G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### G.1.1 ESHR: This component aims to ensure that the programme achieves desirable, sustainable outcomes by managing environmental and social risks. The programme will be required to conduct an initial environmental and social risk screening at the concept note stage of the project cycle. All projects will be required to undertake an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) as part of their project cycle.

### G.1.2 The programme is classified as Category B. This indicates that the nature of the investments is likely to have minimal or negligible environmental and social impacts and risks, or if there are potential adverse environmental and social impacts, they can be managed by mitigation measures identified in an ESMP. ESHR will serve as a guide for National Designated Entities (NDEs), which will work with accredited entities to ensure that the programme is implemented in accordance with the requirements of this component during the five years of programme implementation (PI). The ESHR policy will be reviewed and updated every two years, or when necessary. The NDE will be responsible for ensuring compliance with the ESHR policy.

- Detailed guidance on the specific E&S risks associated with each type of eligible investment.
- This guidance will be developed in consultation with accredited entities and other relevant stakeholders to ensure that it is comprehensive and useful.
- This guidance will be made available on the GCF website, where it will be an additional resource for accredited entities.

### G.1.3 The programme will use the ESHR policy as a guide for the development of the ESMPs required by the ESHR requirements outlined in the ESHR policy. Accredited entities are expected to develop and implement their own ESMPs, which should be consistent with the ESHR policy.

### G.1.4 The programme will use the ESHR policy as a guide for the development of the ESMPs required by the ESHR requirements outlined in the ESHR policy. Accredited entities are expected to develop and implement their own ESMPs, which should be consistent with the ESHR policy.

### G.1.5 Although E&S risk has not been identified as a significant issue for the programme, it is likely to be included in the GCF policies on eligibility, fiduciary risk management, and safeguards. The programme will therefore need to ensure that its ESMPs are consistent with these policies.

## EAS-related components, notably by contracting: (i) a National Energy Consultant to work with each selected EAS and (ii) an Implementation Support Consultant for the full cycle of EAS activities.

### G.4.6
The EAS will be implemented through a combination of direct support from GCF accredited entities and managed by the programme with proper early engagement between national stakeholders, including sub-national authorities, to ensure that the programme is properly designed and managed. The inclusion of a working group composed of key stakeholders is essential for ensuring that the programme is well-championed and supported at all levels.

The GCF FMP is managed by the GCF's Office of Integrity, Compliance and Accountability (ICA). Through the ICA, the GCF has developed a comprehensive compliance policy. In both components, the requirements are relevant and necessary to ensure that the programme is implemented in accordance with the GCF's policies.

For more information on the programme, please contact:

- **Through a wide-rise notice** [70-405-023], via telephone [686-029-1777].
- **Or visit**: [Office of Integrity Compliance and Accountability](https://www.gcfund.org/ica)

Building on the Development Bank's (DB) experience in implementing programmes, the DB can also be used to implement the programme. Specific arrangements are needed to ensure compatibility directly with the GCF. More information about the GCF will be provided in the following link.

### G.4.7
The EAS was developed in close consultation with relevant national stakeholders. The programme is designed to support the implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation activities based on the needs identified through the programme. The new approach integrated local-level interventions with NAPs, which enables the programme to be more effective and sustainable.

Consultations are expected between April and May 2023, which will improve the programme's design and ensure that it is aligned with national priorities and objectives. The programme will also provide information on how to best prepare for future climate change impacts. To enhance the potential of based interventions, the programme preparation has already been initiated.

For more information on the programme, please contact:

- **Through a wide-rise notice** [70-405-023], via telephone [686-029-1777].
- **Or visit**: [Office of Integrity Compliance and Accountability](https://www.gcfund.org/ica)

## Q.8. Standardised engagement will be strongly important long-term programme implementation. The GCF provides guidelines for the disbursement process, the design of the project, and the monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework. These guidelines ensure that the project is implemented in a manner consistent with the principles of environmental sustainability, gender equality, and social inclusion.

### Q.8.1.
The design of the programme has been informed by a detailed Gender Assessment, which focused on our key groups as identified through research per country. It involves persons with disabilities (PWD), the LGBTQIA+ community, and women. The assessment was conducted in collaboration with local organisations that focus on inclusion and diversity services, although the ways in which this inclusion may manifest for each group can vary significantly. The design of the programme has been developed to ensure that it is inclusive and accessible by the additional barrier experienced primarily in Belize and Jamaica is the weak enabling environment for the use and uptake of new technologies. This can be a barrier as persons may be unaware of the current available technologies, or they may not have access to them. The current challenge on these technologies may not be the most conducive to the sustainable implementation of the programme.

### Q.8.2.
An additional and unique barrier facing indigenous people is that their traditional homes may not be compatible with new technologies. This can be a significant barrier as it limits the ability to use new technologies effectively. The barriers include those shaped by law and discrimination and that take the form of necessitated services.

### G.3.4. Through the Gender Action Plan (GAP), a programme will seek the following gender needs/ways to be programmed, governance and participation:
- **Gender-based budgeting** founded on policies, the utilities and other benefits of gender equality.
- **Gender-responsive planning** that is inclusive and participatory.
- **Gender-responsive budgeting** that is responsive to the needs of women and men.

#### (H) Enhancing the enabling environment
The programme will support activities aimed at enhancing better understanding of gender and social inclusion issues. The context of the energy, finance and climate change sectors will be promoted for these efforts. This will include the development of a gender-responsive budgeting framework that is responsive to the needs of women and men. It will also promote the implementation of gender-responsive budgeting in the energy sector.

#### (I) Enhancing awareness and understanding
The programme will support the development of marketing materials and tools to enhance the understanding of the benefits of gender equality. These materials will be developed by the team and will include a range of activities that are aimed at increasing the understanding of the benefits of gender equality. The programme will also promote the use of these materials in the energy sector.

#### (J) Enhancing the enabling environment
The programme will support the development of marketing materials and tools to enhance the understanding of the benefits of gender equality. These materials will be developed by the team and will include a range of activities that are aimed at increasing the understanding of the benefits of gender equality. The programme will also promote the use of these materials in the energy sector.

## F

It will be difficult to identify and develop subproject opportunities that would be eligible by the existing GCF funding modalities. It is best addressed through the FA component and a process has (already) been initiated for this purpose. The FA component will provide an opportunity to address the needs of countries with limited capacity to access GCF resources directly, including small island developing states, least developed countries, and landlocked developing countries. This approach will also be useful in addressing the needs of countries that are not eligible under the existing modalities but have been identified as having high potential for subproject opportunities. The FA component will be managed by an entity designated by the GCF Secretariat.

### G.3 Financial management and procurement (approx. 600 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Legal requirements

**G.3.1:** CDB will sign a standard agreement with each of the National Executing Entities that will govern the use of funds for subproject implementation. The agreements will include standard CDB conditions and requirements as well as any specific conditions required by GCF to ensure compliance with the CDB and the GCF policies. The process of preparing such standard agreements will be led by the CDB.

#### Fiduciary standards

**G.3.2:** Each entity (utility as an National Executing Entity) will bear overall responsibility for ensuring that all subprojects are implemented in accordance with fiduciary standards and requirements. This includes, but is not limited to, ensuring that procurement processes are conducted in a transparent manner and that procurement decisions are based on objective criteria. The process of preparing such standard agreements will be led by the CDB.

## F

### Consulting Services: Not part of the purchasing utilities as a faster path to GCF Programmatic Readiness and Accreditation (PRA) is preferred.

Most of the activities pertaining to services under the FA component will be carried out by the accredited entities, which will be selected through an open and transparent process. The services will include but not limited to:

- Provision of technical assistance for the development of the programmatic approaches.
- The conduct of audits/assessments with respect to a programme action plan under GCF programmes.
- The conduct of audits/assessments with respect to a programme action plan under GCF programmes.

### Audit

All activities undertaken under this component will be managed according to the International Accreditation Panel (IAP) rules and standards, which are endorsed by the GCF Board. The IAP is an independent body that provides audit services to the GCF. The IAP will:

- Select a team of directly contracted independent external auditors to perform a partial financial audit of the programme and project activities.
- Submit the audit report to the Executive Committee.

### G.I. Disclosure of funding proposals

The accredited entity is required to make its funding proposal available to the GCF Secretariat. VPA and RFA will reflect GCF rules with respect to disclosure of information. This includes:

- The disclosure of the funding proposal.
- The disclosure of the funding proposal, including any amendments, may be disclosed by the GCF, as an element is being reviewed in confidence.

### No confidential information

The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including, as amended, may be disclosed by the GCF, as an element is being reviewed in confidence. This includes:

- All copies for internal use of the GCF or which contain portions marked accordingly, along with any other documents prepared by the accredited entity.
- All copies for disclosure to the GCF Secretariat.

  - Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)

- **Annex 8**: Summary of institutional and sub-national government plan
- **Annex 9**: Gender assessment and programme-level action plan (mandatory)
- **Annex 10**: Length of the disbursement, loan and insurance (applicable)
- **Annex 11**: Financial risk management plan (mandatory)
- **Annex 12**: AEI for project (mandatory)
- **Annex 13**: Co-financing commitment letter (applicable)
- **Annex 14**: Team visit report including a detailed implementation schedule, etc. (applicable)
- **Annex 15**: Other annexes as applicable

### H.3. Optional annexes

- **Annex 16**: Multi-country programming or information (applicable)
- **Annex 17**: Multi-country programming annex - information (applicable)
- **Annex 18**: Annex on due diligence of evaluation report for projects based in up-scaling or replicating a past project
- **Annex 19**: Procedures for obtaining permission by third parties exercising rights under applicable laws
- **Annex 20**: First-time AM/CFP/RVC assessment (applicable)
- **Annex 21**: Update on the Green Climate Fund (GCF) accreditation and their monitoring and reporting on the negation and cross-cutting approaches

* Annex 22 is mandatory for negotiation and cross-cutting impacts.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP275

## Proposed name
Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase 1) Feasibility Study

## Accredited entity
Caribbean Development Bank

## Country(ies)
Barbados, Belize and Jamaica

## Project/programme name
Regional Energy Efficiency Building Programme

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

This scoping document submitted by the Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) as accredited entity (AE), seeks to establish and scale a regional Integrated Utility Services (IUS) programme that will support energy sector transformation in the Caribbean Small Island Developing States (SIDS). The IUS programme aims at accelerating decarbonisation and enhancing energy resilience. The proposed interventions Phase 1 Feasibility Study will assess the potential for scaling up integrated utility services to provide distributed renewable energy (RE), energy efficiency, and demand-side management solutions.

1. **The RS model** builds on the concept of utilities acting as energy service companies. It is based on the business case that utilities can generate additional revenue by providing energy services beyond electricity supply. The RS model has been successfully implemented in several countries including the Philippines, India, and Kenya. The RS model has also been piloted in Jamaica through a partnership between JPS and CDB to provide energy efficiency solutions to commercial customers.

2. **The programme** is structured around three interrelated components:

   - **Component 1**: This component will support the creation of regulatory frameworks, institutional capacities, and other enabling conditions for the IUS programme. It will also support the development of a business case for the IUS programme.
   - **Component 2**: This component provides a concessional financing facility to the selected countries to support the implementation of the IUS programme. The financing facility will be provided through a combination of grants and low-interest loans.
   - **Component 3**: This component will provide technical assistance to support the development and implementation of the IUS programme.

### 1.2 Assessment Criteria

The assessment will be based on the following criteria:

- Relevance: Is the proposed project relevant to the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF)?
- Potential for Impact: Does the proposed project have the potential to achieve significant greenhouse gas emission reductions and/or climate change resilience benefits?
- Efficiency: Is the proposed project designed in a way that maximizes the use of GCF resources?
- Sustainability: Does the proposed project include measures to ensure that its benefits will be sustained after GCF funding ends?
- Coherence: Is the proposed project coherent with other initiatives and programmes at the national, regional, and international levels?
- Synergies: Does the proposed project have the potential to generate additional benefits through synergies with other projects and programmes?
- Gender Equality: Does the proposed project include measures to promote gender equality and empower women?
- Direct Access: Is the proposed project consistent with the direct access modalities of the GCF?
- Readiness: Has the accredited entity demonstrated its readiness to implement the proposed project?

### 1.3 Assessment Methodology

The assessment will be conducted in accordance with the GCF’s Technical Screening Criteria and the relevant guidance provided by the GCF Secretariat.

# Green Climate Fund

## Residual commercial and public sector - covering capital expenditures for carbon capture technology

The residual emissions are expected to be offset at different costs in the 4-to-6-percent range of GDP.

### Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS) technologies

Carbon dioxide is captured from power plants, refineries, and other industrial sources. It can be transported via pipeline or ship to a storage site where it is injected into underground geological formations for long-term storage. The process involves three main steps:

1. **Capture**: CO2 is separated from the emissions produced by power generation.
2. **Transport**: The captured CO2 is transported via pipelines or ships to the storage site.
3. **Storage**: The CO2 is injected deep underground into suitable geological formations.

### Carbon Capture Utilization (CCU)

Carbon dioxide can also be utilized in various industrial processes, such as:

- Manufacturing chemicals and materials
- Enhancing oil recovery through enhanced oil recovery (EOR)
- Producing synthetic fuels

### Carbon Pricing Mechanisms

Carbon pricing mechanisms are essential for incentivizing low-carbon technologies. These include:

- **Carbon Tax**: A tax on carbon emissions to encourage reductions.
- **Cap-and-Trade**: A system where companies are limited in their greenhouse gas emissions and can buy or sell allowances.

### Impact Potential

The proposed CCS projects have the potential to deliver both mitigation and adaptation benefits through the efficient use of fossil fuel exploitation of distributed RE (renewable energy) and other demand-side measures.

### Scale: Medium

In summary, CCS technologies are a critical component in achieving deep decarbonization. They can significantly reduce emissions from industrial processes and power generation while providing a pathway for utilizing captured CO2 in various applications.

**Page 134**
---

energy services in Belarus, Belize and Jamaica. These three Caribbean SIDS face mounting climate risks such as increased storms, prolonged droughts, sea level rise and flooding, average temperature increases and changes to precipitation patterns. The programme responds to these challenges by combining biodiversity conservation with sustainable development, addressing the root causes of climate change and regulatory barriers.

The programme will support the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process in each country, through the formulation of national adaptation plans and strategies, engaging stakeholders at all levels. It will also support the integration of gender considerations into NAP processes and the development of gender-responsive policies and programmes. The programme will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 13 on climate action, and SDG 5 on gender equality.

The programme will be implemented over five years (2021-2026) with an estimated budget of USD 4 million. It will be funded through a combination of grants and loans from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) and other sources. The programme will be implemented by the Ministry of Environment in each country, with technical support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP).

The programme will be implemented in three phases:

1. **Phase 1: Preparation** - This phase will involve the development of a comprehensive national adaptation plan for each country, including an assessment of vulnerabilities and risks, as well as the identification of priority actions.
2. **Phase 2: Implementation** - During this phase, the programme will support the implementation of the national adaptation plans, including the provision of technical assistance and capacity building to local governments and communities.
3. **Phase 3: Monitoring and Evaluation** - This final phase will involve the monitoring and evaluation of the programme's progress, as well as the development of a framework for long-term sustainability.

The programme is expected to result in significant improvements in the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change, as well as enhanced capacity for sustainable development. It is also expected to contribute to the achievement of the SDGs and the Paris Agreement goals.

# Green Code

## Agreements and commitments with the goal of cutting the road accident fatality rate in half by 2030

Agreements to cut the road accident fatality rate in half by 2030 are being signed throughout the world, including those on safety belts, speed limits, and alcohol and drug testing. These agreements are expected to be beneficial for all countries, regardless of their level of income, as they can be implemented with minimal resources. The agreements also include provisions for monitoring progress towards achieving these goals.

### Risk to traffic safety

There are several risks to traffic safety. First, the agreements provide only partial and weak regulatory measures, especially in some developing countries where the enforcement of existing regulations is weak. Second, the agreements do not address the root causes of road accidents, such as unsafe infrastructure and vehicle design. Third, the agreements do not include provisions for monitoring progress towards achieving these goals.

### To mitigate the risk of defaults at higher rates than expected

To mitigate the risk of defaults at higher rates than expected, or even prevent them, the following measures should be taken:

- **Enhance transparency**: Enhance transparency by providing clear and consistent information on the implementation of agreements. This can include regular reports on progress towards achieving these goals.
- **Strengthen enforcement**: Strengthen enforcement of existing regulations to ensure that they are being followed. This can include increased penalties for violations, as well as improved training for law enforcement officers.
- **Promote public awareness**: Promote public awareness of the importance of traffic safety by conducting campaigns and providing education on safe driving practices.

### Paradigm shift potential

The agreements provide an opportunity to promote a paradigm shift in traffic safety. This can include:

- **Encourage innovation**: Encourage innovation in vehicle design, road infrastructure, and enforcement strategies.
- **Promote collaboration**: Promote collaboration between governments, international organizations, and the private sector to develop new solutions.

## Scale: Medium

The agreements provide an opportunity to promote a paradigm shift in traffic safety. This can include:

- **Encourage innovation**: Encourage innovation in vehicle design, road infrastructure, and enforcement strategies.
- **Promote collaboration**: Promote collaboration between governments, international organizations, and the private sector to develop new solutions.

# regulatory bodies

However, these alone may not be sufficient to reconcile the differences between bottom-up distributed energy approaches and top-down utility scale programs.

## a. Distributed generation

Distributed generation can be integrated with existing planning processes and is often seen as a means of addressing local reliability issues. It is integrated into wholesale systems planning, forecasting, and dispatch. However, it may not be distributed across advanced metering infrastructure to address a broader set of policy and regulatory goals.

### b. Benchmarking

Distributed generation presents an opportunity for the use of climate-related benchmarks and other more granular metrics to track severity, particularly in areas prone to natural disasters.

### c. Incentives

Many Caribbean households in MENAs have historically lacked access to reliable electricity. Distributed energy resources (DER) can provide a means of addressing this gap through more regular participation in the energy landscape.

## b. Large-scale generation

Large-scale generation is likely to continue to be the most cost-effective option for meeting long-term demand growth and ensuring grid reliability.

### i. Offshore wind

Offshore wind projects present an opportunity for large-scale renewable energy development that is typically underwritten by high capacity payments and long-term power purchase agreements (PPAs).

### ii. Local communities

Under many utility-scale RE projects, which are typically led by the private sector, local communities may be excluded from the benefits of these energy assets.

## c. Technology

Technology can play a significant role in facilitating the integration of distributed generation into existing systems. This includes:

- **Data management**: Data management is essential to facilitate a dynamic and adaptable grid that can respond to changes in demand and supply.
- **Energy storage**: Energy storage can be used to smooth out fluctuations in renewable energy output, particularly when combined with other DER technologies.

### i. Energy storage

Energy storage can play an important role in facilitating the integration of distributed generation into existing systems. This includes:

- **Data management**: Data management is essential to facilitate a dynamic and adaptable grid that can respond to changes in demand and supply.
- **Energy storage**: Energy storage can be used to smooth out fluctuations in renewable energy output, particularly when combined with other DER technologies.

## 1.4 Sustainable development potential

### Scale: Medium to high

The funding proposal outlines a range of specific sustainable development co-benefits, many of which are aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These include:

- **Environmental**: The RE projects support a shift away from fossil fuels and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. This is consistent with SDG 7: Affordable and Clean Energy.
- **Economic**: The RE projects provide local employment opportunities, particularly in areas where the energy sector is a significant part of the economy.

### b. Environmental co-benefits

The RE projects support a shift away from fossil fuels and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. This is consistent with SDG 7: Affordable and Clean Energy.

# Green Action Plan

## Monitoring public ecological disruption or soil use conflicts

This particularly pertains to land-use conflicts such as herbicides, where land-use conflicts limit the feasibility of large-scale agriculture.

### To address these issues:

- **Initiate** a transition to cleaner agricultural generation.
- **Promote** sustainable farming practices and organic agriculture.
- **Encourage** local food systems and community-supported agriculture. These methods are environmentally friendly and consistent with regional economic development.

## Economic benefits

The Green Action Plan presents a credible pathway to delivering human health benefits, both societal and commercial, to the consumer side, in an environment-friendly manner.

### Benefits include:

- **Reduced** environmental impact.
- **Increased** sustainability.
- **Enhanced** community resilience.
- **Improved** public health outcomes through reduced exposure to harmful chemicals and improved access to healthy foods.

## Social considerations

The Green Action Plan aims to integrate household-level actions to clean up the environment. This is particularly relevant in the Global South, where environmental degradation is often linked to poverty and inequality.

### Benefits include:

- **Reduced** environmental impact.
- **Increased** sustainability.
- **Enhanced** community resilience.
- **Improved** public health outcomes through reduced exposure to harmful chemicals and improved access to healthy foods.

## Health benefits

Herbicides, along with other substances, can also be linked to human health concerns. The Green Action Plan aims to reduce the use of these substances by promoting sustainable agriculture practices that benefit both people and the environment.

### Benefits include:

- **Reduced** exposure to harmful chemicals.
- **Improved** public health outcomes through reduced exposure to harmful chemicals and improved access to healthy foods.

## Gender considerations

The Green Action Plan aims to promote gender equality in agriculture. This includes addressing gender-based barriers to women's participation in agricultural decision-making, as well as promoting equal opportunities for women and men in the sector.

### Benefits include:

- **Increased** gender equality.
- **Improved** public health outcomes through reduced exposure to harmful chemicals and improved access to healthy foods.

## Youth considerations

The Green Action Plan aims to promote youth engagement in agriculture. This includes addressing barriers to youth participation in agricultural decision-making, as well as promoting equal opportunities for young people in the sector.

### Benefits include:

- **Increased** youth engagement.
- **Improved** public health outcomes through reduced exposure to harmful chemicals and improved access to healthy foods.

## Conclusion

The Green Action Plan presents a comprehensive approach to addressing environmental challenges, promoting sustainable agriculture, and enhancing human health. With appropriate attention to gender and equity, the plan can contribute significantly to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and other international commitments.

# 1.5 Needs of the recipient

## a.
Recipient countries face numerous challenges that impede their participation in, and benefit from, the implementation of HBP and similar programmes. These include weak institutional capacity, inadequate infrastructure, lack of human resources, insufficient financial resources, weak regulatory frameworks, and events such as natural disasters, food crises, and droughts. These factors contribute to high levels of poverty and vulnerability among recipient countries.

## b.
Directly linked to middle-income countries, this section refers to additional monitoring mechanisms. This also applies to countries that have been identified by the Programme as having significant potential for future participation in the Programme. Directly linked countries are those that have been identified by the Programme as having significant potential for future participation in the Programme.

## c.
Importantly, HBP programmes target persistent equity gaps within their countries. These include gender equality, social inclusion, and poverty reduction. The programme also targets vulnerable groups such as children, people with disabilities, LGBTIQA+, migrants, and indigenous peoples. People who are the beneficiaries of these programmes are those who have been identified by the Programme as having significant potential for future participation in the Programme.

## d.
The HBP programme also integrates a social inclusion and gender framework. Through this framework, the programme ensures that all participants are treated equally regardless of their race, ethnicity, gender, sexual orientation, disability, or other characteristics. The programme also includes specific measures to ensure that women and girls have equal access to the benefits of the Programme.

## e.
The HBP states that the geographic target is limited to three countries in this first round of implementation. This is due to resource constraints and the need for a phased approach to implementation. The programme will be implemented in a phased manner, with each phase building on the success of the previous one.

## f.
The programme responds directly to structural vulnerabilities in climate-exposed food systems. These include weak infrastructure, inadequate access to finance, and limited capacity to adapt to climate change. The model is designed to address these challenges by providing technical assistance and financial support to countries that are most vulnerable to climate change. This will enable them to build resilience and improve their ability to withstand future shocks.

## g.
The HBP assesses the needs of the recipient as high.

# Introduction

In accordance with GCF policy guidance and standards, the gender statement is here as a critical element of the project proposal to ensure how the project will addressing its impact on gender equality and empowerment. The project has been designed to address gender issues in the target areas. The project will be implemented by the implementing entity and supervised by the executing entity.

The Carbon Development Bank (CDB) is the process of implementing the "Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (SUDS)" support Energy Sector Transformation in the Republic of Maldives. The SUDS project aims to enhance the efficiency, reliability, and sustainability of the energy sector through the deployment of renewable energy technologies.

The Phase of the project will have five following Implementing Committees (IMCs) that will be responsible for the implementation of the project. The IMCs will be formed from the local government and the private sector. The IMC will be responsible for the implementation of the project and the monitoring of the project's progress.

The GCF is committed to measuring the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation risks. The GCF Gender Policy was updated in 2019, and the gender policy is aligned with the GCF Gender Policy. The GCF is committed to ensuring that all projects are implemented in a manner that is consistent with the objectives which are as follows:

- Ensure that gender equality is integrated into the project design.
- Ensure that gender equality is integrated into the project implementation.
- Ensure that gender equality is integrated into the project monitoring and evaluation.

The project will be designed to address gender issues by integrating gender-specific measures such as social risks and climate change adaptation measures. The project will also include gender-specific measures such as social risks and climate change adaptation measures.

The project's contribution to the UNDP project will aim:

- To enhance the capacity of local governments to implement sustainable development goals.
- To enhance the capacity of local governments to implement sustainable development goals.
- To enhance the capacity of local governments to implement sustainable development goals.

An enabling environment among GCF stakeholders built on existing policy commitments, order achieve gender equality and empowerment. The project will also include measures such as gender-specific measures such as social risks and climate change adaptation measures. The project will also include measures such as gender-specific measures such as social risks and climate change adaptation measures.

# The Assessment

The Assessment builds on the precedent work of the baseline assessment and seeks to provide a deeper understanding of future potential impacts (both water-related and otherwise) from climate change in the U.S. and globally. It also provides an update on the current state of knowledge, as well as identify barriers to uptake.

## Objectives

- The general framework agreement for the GCF proposal.
- The need for a comprehensive assessment of adaptation needs and gaps.
- The need for gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation tools that may be needed.
- The need for a comprehensive assessment of climate change impacts on water resources, including the potential for water-related disasters to impact the IWRM model implementation in an equitable manner.

## Limitations

This assessment utilized a mixed-method approach in a desk-based research and stakeholder engagement. However, the process was still limited by a few factors including:

- No extensive data collection and analysis activities which results in the use of data at a global scale.
- The need for more detailed information on the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.
- The need to identify and assess the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.

The assessment was conducted as part of the Climate Change Adaptation Program (CCAP) of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The CCAP is a global program that aims to support countries in their efforts to adapt to the adverse effects of climate change. The assessment was conducted by a team of experts from the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC).

## Challenges

As it stands, there are several key limitations that affect the baseline assessment:

- The need for more detailed information on the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.
- The need to identify and assess the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.

The assessment was conducted as part of the Climate Change Adaptation Program (CCAP) of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The CCAP is a global program that aims to support countries in their efforts to adapt to the adverse effects of climate change. The assessment was conducted by a team of experts from the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC).

## Limitations

This assessment utilized a mixed-method approach in a desk-based research and stakeholder engagement. However, the process was still limited by a few factors including:

- No extensive data collection and analysis activities which results in the use of data at a global scale.
- The need for more detailed information on the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.
- The need to identify and assess the potential impacts of climate change on water resources.

# Financial Barriers

## Difficulties in accessing funds from financial institutions

- Access to funding is a major barrier for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).
- Banks are wary of providing loans due to perceived risks.
- Limited access to capital hinders growth.

## High costs associated with financial services

- Fees for financial services can be prohibitive.
- Costs related to compliance and regulation add to the burden.

## Lack of awareness about financial products

- Entrepreneurs may not be aware of available financial products.
- Limited knowledge about financial management tools.

## Lack of collateral

- Many SMEs lack the necessary collateral required by banks.
- This limits their ability to secure loans.

# Gender Mainstreaming in the IUS Project

Gender mainstreaming will help to ensure that:

- The project is designed and implemented with a gender perspective.
- It addresses the specific needs of women and men.
- It promotes equal opportunities for all beneficiaries.

## Objectives of Gender Mainstreaming

1. To integrate gender equality into the project's objectives, activities, and outcomes.
2. To identify and address the specific barriers faced by women in accessing financial services.
3. To promote the economic empowerment of women through targeted interventions.

## Strategies for Gender Mainstreaming

- Conduct gender assessments to understand the needs of both men and women.
- Develop and implement gender-responsive policies and procedures.
- Provide training and capacity-building activities to enhance gender equality.
- Monitor and evaluate progress towards gender goals.

## Key to ensuring the project meets its objectives

The key to ensuring the project meets its objectives is to identify the existing components of those who will be charged in delivering and supporting the project. This includes identifying and addressing the needs of different groups, including women and men, and developing targeted strategies to support them. This approach will help to ensure that the project is inclusive and effective in promoting gender equality.

# Gender Mainstreaming in the IUS Project

Gender mainstreaming will not be possible without a gender perspective and an analysis with intersecting dimensions such as class, ethnicity, race, age, disability, sexual orientation, and so on.

Gender mainstreaming is the process of assessing the implications for women and men of any planned action, including legislation, policies or programs, in all areas and at all levels. It is not just a matter of addressing issues of concern to women only. The ultimate goal of gender mainstreaming is to achieve gender equality.

## Indigenous People and Communities

Bringing together the unique cultural perspectives of indigenous peoples is an important aspect of gender mainstreaming. The following are some key considerations:

- Engaging with indigenous communities to ensure that their voices are heard.
- Recognizing and respecting traditional knowledge, values, and practices.
- Ensuring that policies and programs are culturally appropriate and responsive.

Persons with disabilities are another special group of interest to be considered within the setting of gender mainstreaming. The needs of persons with disabilities have often been overlooked in development projects. They have been under-represented in health care, education, employment, and other areas due to the lack of consideration for their specific needs. In addition, they have often been excluded from decision-making processes that affect their lives.

# Barbados

## 3.1.1 Policy Environment

Barbados is a Caribbean island in the energy sector. Its leadership has seen progress in the development of renewable energy over the past decade. The government has set a target to achieve 29% renewable energy by 2030. This goal was outlined in the National Energy Policy, which was revised in November 2018. It includes 45 action items aimed at improving energy efficiency and reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 30%.

The Department of Energy has been actively involved in promoting renewable energy and energy efficiency. One program being implemented is a "Roof for Rent" project that allows homeowners to install solar photovoltaic systems on their roofs without any upfront costs. This initiative aims to improve the social and economic well-being of citizens by providing access to affordable, reliable, and sustainable energy. The government has also been promoting gender equality in the energy sector through various initiatives aimed at enhancing women's participation in decision-making processes and improving the standard of living and quality of life. Through these programs, Barbados is working towards achieving its goal of a more inclusive and equitable society.

Take for example the Barbados National Energy Policy which establishes the roadmap for the development of renewable energy sources on the island. The policy aims to increase the use of renewable energy by 2030. It also has the different ways of energy by gender would be considered and of having a more inclusive approach in the development of policies and programs related to the advancement of Energy and Gender within Barbados, and the gender concerns and rating of each sector.

## Solar Water Heating

Solar water heating (SWH) is optimized for its use in solar water heating to households and businesses, with over 50,000 water heaters installed (1984). SWH technology combines the use of a solar collector and a storage tank. The system can be used to heat water for bathing, cleaning, cooking, and washing. Outside of the household, solar water heating has been used in commercial applications such as hotels, restaurants, and swimming pools. Solar water heating is also used as a domestic hot water source.

The technology has been around since 1984 in Barbados (1984). In Barbados, it accounts for 18% of the population, according to the 2010 Census. This group was the production (arms) vulnerability to diseases such as malaria and cholera. The use of solar water heating is a cost-effective way to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve indoor air quality. It also reduces the need for electricity and natural gas, which can be used to heat water. Solar water heating systems are typically installed on the roof or side of a building. They consist of a solar collector, a storage tank, and a pump. The solar collector captures sunlight and converts it into heat, which is then transferred to the storage tank. The storage tank holds the heated water until it is needed.

The benefits of solar water heating include:

- Reduced energy costs
- Improved indoor air quality
- Reduced greenhouse gas emissions
- Increased comfort and convenience

These benefits can allow the elderly who have fixed incomes to continue to support positive health outcomes, such as bathing. This can also help to reduce the need for further investments in their diet (particularly, healthy food consumption) which can be a barrier to healthy aging.

## Rooftop Solar Photovoltaics

Rooftop solar photovoltaics (PV) is a technology that converts sunlight into electricity using photovoltaic cells. PV systems are typically installed on rooftops and can provide electricity for homes and businesses. Rooftop solar PV is a cost-effective way to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve indoor air quality. It also reduces the need for electricity from the grid, which can be expensive.

The benefits of rooftop solar PV include:

- Reduced energy costs
- Improved indoor air quality
- Reduced greenhouse gas emissions
- Increased comfort and convenience

Rooftop solar PV systems are typically installed on rooftops and can provide electricity for homes and businesses. The systems consist of photovoltaic cells, an inverter, and a mounting system. The photovoltaic cells convert sunlight into direct current (DC) electricity, which is then converted to alternating current (AC) electricity by the inverter. The mounting system secures the PV cells to the roof.

Rooftop solar PV systems can be used to power homes, businesses, and other buildings. They are typically installed on rooftops, but can also be installed on ground-mounted structures. Rooftop solar PV systems can provide electricity for homes and businesses, as well as reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

Another issue of the institutional level is the absence of legislation requiring buildings to implement IUIM technologies such as solar water heating and roof-top wind turbines on their premises. This has led to a situation where some people are not aware of these technologies or do not have the financial means to install them. The Barbados Government has responded by providing incentives for individuals who may be willing to install and use solar PV, solar water heating and roof-top wind turbines. The Government has also established a Renewable Energy Fund with an initial endowment of BBD $10 million, with an increasing fund for renewal loans. This is in addition to the Government's commitment to provide incentives for individuals who wish to install renewable energy systems on their premises.

In addition, the absence of legislation requiring the use of efficient air conditioning and lighting (IEA) also remains a barrier to the Barbados Integrated Energy Approach (BIEA). The Government has been working with the IEA to develop a national standard for energy efficiency in buildings. This is an important step towards reducing greenhouse gas emissions and improving energy security.

Notably, the Government of Barbados has been making strides to implement projects that include the use of renewable energy technologies such as solar PV and wind turbines. The Government has also established a Renewable Energy Fund with an initial endowment of BBD $10 million, with an increasing fund for renewal loans. This is in addition to the Government's commitment to provide incentives for individuals who wish to install renewable energy systems on their premises.

The implementation of Electric Mobility via Public Transportation Project is one such example. The project aims to replace existing diesel buses with electric buses and to establish a charging infrastructure that can support the operation of these vehicles. This project is expected to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 50% over the next five years.

# Home Ownership Providing Energy Project (HOPE) Project

The Home Ownership Providing Energy Project (HOPE) Project seeks to protect households from energy poverty and improve their living conditions by providing them with solar photovoltaic systems and energy efficiency measures. Households that are vulnerable and those who are more susceptible to poverty and those in poverty (unemployed) are the target groups for this project. The HOPE Project Consortium for young people, in this context, aims from underprivileged families.

More generally, vulnerability is further exacerbated by poor-quality housing such as repurposed shipping containers or makeshift shelters built with scrap materials. As an example, the passing of Hurricane Ida in 2021 with reports including 533 deaths and $64 billion in damages highlighted the need for improved housing standards.

## Economic

Due to its geopolitical position i.e., Latin America and the Caribbean, Barbados is often exposed to natural disasters such as hurricanes and earthquakes. This has led to a high level of poverty and unemployment. Consequently, the number of people who are unemployed and underemployed is increasing. The government is trying to improve the living conditions for its citizens by providing them with better housing and employment opportunities.

The HOPE Project aims to provide solar photovoltaic systems and energy efficiency measures to households in Barbados. This will help to reduce their vulnerability to natural disasters and improve their living conditions. The project will also create jobs and stimulate economic growth.

The HOPE Project Consortium for young people, in this context, aims from underprivileged families.

## Table

| Category                          | Description                                                                                       |
|----------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Sustainable Energy**           | Promotes the use of renewable energy to reduce carbon emissions and improve air quality.         |
| **Energy Efficiency**            | Helps households save money on their energy bills by reducing their energy consumption.          |
| **Economic Development**         | Creates jobs and stimulates economic growth through the installation of solar photovoltaic systems.|
| **Disaster Resilience**          | Improves the resilience of households to natural disasters by providing them with reliable sources of energy. |

## The HOPE Project Consortium

The HOPE Project Consortium is a group of organizations that work together to provide solar photovoltaic systems and energy efficiency measures to households in Barbados. The consortium includes:

- **Sustainable Energy** - Promotes the use of renewable energy to reduce carbon emissions and improve air quality.
- **Energy Efficiency** - Helps households save money on their energy bills by reducing their energy consumption.
- **Economic Development** - Creates jobs and stimulates economic growth through the installation of solar photovoltaic systems.
- **Disaster Resilience** - Improves the resilience of households to natural disasters by providing them with reliable sources of energy.

The HOPE Project Consortium for young people, in this context, aims from underprivileged families.

## Conclusion

The HOPE Project is a comprehensive approach to addressing energy poverty and improving living conditions for vulnerable households in Barbados. By providing solar photovoltaic systems and energy efficiency measures, the project seeks to reduce vulnerability to natural disasters, improve economic prospects, and enhance overall quality of life.

# Belize

Emerging countries are highly concerned with the effects of climate change and natural disasters. Belize is no exception. The country has been actively working to mitigate the impacts of climate change and reduce large-scale environmental disasters. In 2015, the government of Belize passed the Climate Change Act (CCA) to address these issues. The CCA aims to integrate climate change adaptation into national policies and programs. The government also established a Green Climate Fund (GCF) in 2017 with an initial USD 3 million contribution from the country's budget. This fund is designed to support projects that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and promote sustainable development.

Belize has a high poverty rate, which affects its ability to invest in climate change mitigation efforts. The country's GDP per capita was $4,629 in 2017, making it one of the poorest countries in Latin America. According to the World Bank, Belize had an inflation rate of 3.8% in 2021 and a poverty rate of 45%. The country also has high levels of inequality, with the top 10% of the population earning more than 27 times the income of the bottom 10%.

The government is working to address these issues by implementing policies that promote economic growth and reduce poverty. Belize has made significant progress in recent years, with GDP growth rates of 3.5% in 2018 and 4.6% in 2019. The country also has a relatively low unemployment rate, at 7.5% in 2019.

Despite these challenges, Belize is making significant progress in addressing climate change and promoting sustainable development. The government's commitment to the issue is evident in its policies and programs, which aim to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and promote sustainable economic growth.
