# Policy Environment

## Renewable Energy Targets

The policy environment for renewable energy has evolved significantly over time. Early policies were often driven by environmental concerns and aimed to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. However, recent years have seen a shift towards policies that focus on economic benefits such as job creation and increased tax revenues.

### Key Policy Developments

- **Renewable Energy Targets**: Many countries have set targets for renewable energy adoption. These targets vary in scope and ambition but generally aim to increase the share of renewable energy in the overall energy mix.
- **Feed-in Tariffs**: This policy mechanism guarantees a fixed price for the electricity generated from renewable sources over a specified period. It has been successful in promoting renewable energy adoption in many countries.

### Policy Instruments

#### Renewable Energy Targets (RETs)

- **Description**: Mandatory targets set by governments for the amount of renewable energy that must be produced.
- **Examples**: Australia's Renewable Energy Target and the European Union's Renewable Energy Directive.
- **Impact**: Encourages investment in renewable energy projects and increases the overall share of renewables in the energy mix.

#### Feed-in Tariffs (FITs)

- **Description**: A policy mechanism that provides a fixed price for electricity generated from renewable sources over a specified period.
- **Examples**: Germany's Renewable Energy Sources Act (EEG) and Spain's Royal Decree 661/2007.
- **Impact**: Promotes investment in renewable energy projects by providing a stable income stream for producers.

#### Net Metering

- **Description**: Allows consumers to sell excess electricity generated from their own renewable sources back to the grid.
- **Examples**: California's net metering policies and Germany's Renewable Energy Sources Act (EEG).
- **Impact**: Encourages residential and small-scale commercial adoption of renewable energy systems by allowing consumers to offset their electricity bills.

#### Energy Efficiency Policies

- **Description**: Policies aimed at reducing energy consumption through various measures such as building codes, appliance standards, and education.
- **Examples**: The U.S. Department of Energy's Appliance and Equipment Standards Program and the European Union's Ecodesign Directive.
- **Impact**: Reduces overall energy demand and helps to lower greenhouse gas emissions by promoting the use of more efficient technologies.

### National Energy Policy

- **Description**: Comprehensive policies that cover various aspects of energy production, consumption, and regulation.
- **Examples**: The U.S. Energy Policy Act of 2005 and the European Union's Energy Efficiency Directive.
- **Impact**: Provides a framework for long-term energy planning and helps to ensure the sustainability of the energy sector.

### Figures and Tables

#### Figure 3-12: Energy and Gender Policy Environment for Belize

| Sector                  | Description                                                                                       |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Renewable Energy Targets | Mandatory targets set by governments for the amount of renewable energy that must be produced.   |
| Feed-in Tariffs         | Guarantees a fixed price for electricity generated from renewable sources over a specified period. |
| Net Metering             | Allows consumers to sell excess electricity generated from their own renewable sources back to the grid. |
| Energy Efficiency        | Policies aimed at reducing energy consumption through various measures such as building codes, appliance standards, and education. |

#### Table 3-13: Policy Instruments for Renewable Energy

| Instrument                | Description                                                                                       |
|---------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Renewable Energy Targets   | Mandatory targets set by governments for the amount of renewable energy that must be produced.   |
| Feed-in Tariffs           | Guarantees a fixed price for electricity generated from renewable sources over a specified period. |
| Net Metering               | Allows consumers to sell excess electricity generated from their own renewable sources back to the grid. |
| Energy Efficiency          | Policies aimed at reducing energy consumption through various measures such as building codes, appliance standards, and education. |

# General Summary

## The National Energy Action Plan 2019-2030

### Vision

The vision is to create a climate-neutral and well-functioning society where everyone has access to the energy services they need at prices that are fair and affordable.

### Objectives

- **Energy Efficiency**: Reduce energy consumption by 40% compared to 1990 levels.
- **Renewable Energy**: Increase the share of renewable energy in final energy consumption to 32.5%.
- **Energy Poverty**: Reduce the number of households in energy poverty by half.

### Key Actions

- Implement energy efficiency measures in buildings, industry, and transport.
- Promote the use of renewable energy sources.
- Support research and development in low-carbon technologies.
- Enhance energy efficiency in public buildings and infrastructure.
- Improve energy literacy and consumer awareness.
- Foster regional cooperation on energy issues.

## Britain's National Energy Action Plan 2019-2030

### Vision

The vision is to ensure children have access to quality education, healthcare, and a safe environment. The plan aims to provide universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy services by 2030.

### Objectives

- **Energy Access**: Ensure all households have access to electricity and clean cooking solutions.
- **Energy Efficiency**: Improve the efficiency of energy use in homes, schools, and healthcare facilities.
- **Renewable Energy**: Increase the share of renewable energy in the national energy mix to 20% by 2030.

### Key Actions

- Expand access to electricity through mini-grids and off-grid solutions.
- Promote energy-efficient appliances and lighting.
- Support the development of renewable energy projects.
- Enhance energy education and awareness campaigns.
- Foster public-private partnerships for investment in energy infrastructure.
- Strengthen regional cooperation on energy issues.

### Progress

The plan has achieved significant progress, with a 20% increase in access to electricity since its inception. The government is working closely with international partners to meet the targets set out in the plan.

LED Lighting

In 2015, the Energy Unit of the Ministry of Public Service, Energy and Public Utilities launched the LED Lighting Project in partnership with the Department of Education (DepEd) and the Department of Interior and Local Government (DILG). The project aims to replace traditional incandescent bulbs with energy-efficient LED lights. This will help to reduce electricity consumption and improve the quality of lighting in schools, public buildings, and other facilities.

The project is expected to result in significant savings in energy costs and a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions. It will also provide better lighting conditions for students and teachers, improving the learning environment. The use of LED lights will also help to reduce the risk of fire and other accidents caused by traditional incandescent bulbs.

# Solar Water Heating

The Development Finance Corporation (DFC) has partnered with the Banco Bilbao Vizcaya Argentaria (BBVA) to provide a USD 10 million loan to the Development Bank of the Philippines (DBP). This partnership aims to support the development of solar water heating systems in the Philippines. The DFC and BBVA have agreed to provide a USD 5 million loan each, which will be used to invest in SHW as part of their commitment to sustainable energy. The DBP will use this funding to provide loans to households and small businesses for the installation of solar water heaters. This initiative is expected to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by up to one-third over a ten-year period.

With a lot of promotion and awareness efforts for SHW, local governments have been tapping into the benefits of solar water heating systems. The reduction in electricity consumption was achieved through the use of solar energy, which has had a positive impact on the environment. This could lead to increased investment in renewable energy projects by governments and private companies.

The government has introduced a number of initiatives to promote energy efficiency in cooling and refrigeration systems.

### 3.2.2 Housing

Housing quality has been an issue affected in both urban and rural areas of the country, in relation to the implementation/obligation of HEECR. Housing quality is a major concern for many households. The majority of the housing stock in rural areas is substandard. The majority of the housing stock in urban areas suffers from lower (although there is a possibility of it being worse) structural integrity and maintenance issues. The majority of the housing stock in both urban and rural areas has been affected by natural disasters such as floods, earthquakes, and hurricanes. From the MDG Survey it can be seen that a high but significant number of households have reported problems with their housing quality. In urban areas, 58% of households have reported problems with their housing quality and 12% rural households have reported problems with their housing quality. In urban areas, 36% of households have reported problems with their housing quality and 12% rural households have reported problems with their housing quality.

From the survey, 67% of households are headed by men with the remaining 33% headed by women. The majority of these households are headed by people aged between 40-59 years old. The majority of these households are headed by people who have completed primary education or less. The majority of these households are headed by people who have completed secondary education or less.

The issue of housing quality is especially of concern for PHOs in situations where they are living with the elderly and disabled. Another group of major concern, besides of housing quality, and housing maintenance, is the issue of overcrowding. Overcrowding is a major issue that affects the health and well-being of individuals who live in substandard housing. National housing policies and standards do not stipulate adequate RE EE standards for housing. In addition, the lack of enforcement of these standards means that many communities are still living in substandard conditions.

An additional housing-related issue to be considered is the renumbering of the homes (98% of all households are owned by household members with 64.3% of urban households and 77.2% of rural households owning their own home). The suggestion here was that 50% of persons are renting or living in a house owned by another relative. This is not an issue for those who have modified their homes to meet the needs of the elderly, disabled, or other special needs. However, it would be a major concern if the content of the property were to be lost due to the renumbering process. The issue here is that the renumbering process would prevent renters from using their own address. This means the tenant would have to use the landlord's address for all correspondence and other communications. This could cause confusion between renter and property owner and the legal requirements in place as they may require the use of a specific address.

With regard to learning and decision-making about homes, it is noted that 67% of households are owned by household members and some are able to obtain loans from banks or other financial institutions. It is reported that the majority of persons who own their home have modified their property to meet their needs. This can include modifications for the household's energy needs and insulation requirements. Household improvements can also be made to improve the quality of life in the home, such as adding a new bathroom or kitchen. In some cases, this may involve the use of a loan from a bank or other financial institution.

On the opposite spectrum, female-headed households are more likely to rent the same house with household members who were not household members. However, they would be less likely to have modified their home to meet their needs. This is due to the fact that they may not have access to loans and other forms of credit. They also may not have the financial resources to make improvements to their home.

One of the key stakeholders for this project will be the utility BEL (the Executing Entity). They will be charged with executing the project which would entail managing the funds to be allocated and ensuring that the project is executed in a timely manner. BEL will also be responsible for ensuring that the project is executed in accordance with customers' general needs. Noting the work that BEL has undertaken with community groups such as the Women's Bureau, it is expected that BEL would be able to engage with customers and ensure that their needs are met. BEL has been working with the Ministry of Gender Affairs to gain insight into the needs of women and how they can be better served by the utility. ### 3.3.1 Policy environment

Jamaica's renewable energy capacity stood at 17.1% in 2020 with solar and wind contributing to this total. The government has set a target of 20% renewables by 2030, which is expected to be achieved through the use of solar and wind power.

Jamaica's Integrated Resources Plan 2019 led out the country's goal to reach 30% renewable energy by 2037. The plan also includes a target of 50% renewables by 2040, which is expected to be achieved through the use of solar and wind power.

## Jamaica's Long-Term Vision for Sustainable Development

Jamaica's long-term vision for sustainable development is to achieve a globally competitive, inclusive and prosperous society by 2030.

### National Development Plan (NDP)

The NDP provides the framework for achieving this vision through:

- **Economic Growth** - Increasing productivity and competitiveness.
- **Social Progress** - Improving quality of life and reducing inequality.
- **Environmental Sustainability** - Protecting natural resources and promoting sustainable use.

### Vision 2030 Jamaica

Vision 2030 Jamaica is a long-term national development plan that outlines the country's aspirations for the year 2030. It aims to:

- Achieve economic growth.
- Improve social progress.
- Promote environmental sustainability.

## Policies and Strategies

Seven policies were identified in Jamaica: 3 neutral, 3 sensitive, and 3 transformative. The least transformative policies are those that do not address gender equality issues within broader change response and sustainable development pathways. While the most transformative policies are those that explicitly address gender equality issues.

### National Policy for Gender Equality (NPGE)

The NPGE is quite detailed and one of the few countries with a national policy on gender equality. It provides a framework to guide the implementation of gender mainstreaming in all sectors, including:

- **Government Policies** - Ensuring policies are gender-responsive.
- **Legislation** - Promoting gender equality through laws.
- **Institutional Mechanisms** - Establishing mechanisms for monitoring and evaluation.

### Jamaica's Update of Their NDP

Jamaica's update of their NDP was published in 2019. In preparation of the document, a comprehensive gender analysis was conducted to identify areas where gender equality issues need to be addressed. This includes:

- **Economic Empowerment** - Ensuring equal access to economic opportunities.
- **Social Protection** - Providing social safety nets for vulnerable groups.

### Jamaica's National Development Plan (NDP)

The NDP provides a framework for achieving sustainable development by 2030. It aims to:

- Promote inclusive growth.
- Reduce poverty and inequality.
- Protect the environment.

# Gender equality and women’s empowerment

and education: a basis and coherent society; a progressive economy; and maintaining a thriving natural resource environment. The plan recognises inequalities experienced by women and men in Jamaica, and the need to address these issues. It also seeks to address some of the underlying causes of gender inequality, such as unequal power relations within households, or lack of specific reference to gender or women. The same applies to the need for a more balanced approach to the development, creativity and development of products and services. Issues of sexuality and sexual orientation are not specifically addressed.

Jamaica was elected, as of July 2022, to serve on the Executive Board of UN Women for the period 2023-2025. Jamaica has also launched a gender and climate change strategy, which is aligned with the global gender equality agenda. The country has also developed a national gender policy and a national policy on gender-based violence.

## Usage

Gender mainstreaming faces a disproportionate burden of labour compared with men, through their responsibilities taking care of children and other household tasks, in addition to any labour market activities. This can limit women’s ability to participate fully in the labour force and restricts their access to financial assets and resources. Intensive service work can also place a strain on the health and well-being of women.

Gender mainstreaming is an essential approach to ensure that both cost and time savings to communities, especially women who already may be spending a great deal of time on unpaid care work. Gender mainstreaming can have additional benefits in terms of reducing alienation between men, women and children, and improving income generation opportunities for women.

### LED Lighting

In recent years, the Jamaican Public Service (JPS) has been working on energy conservation initiatives to reduce its carbon footprint. The JPS has replaced over 100,000 high-pressure sodium (HPS) lamps with LED lighting. Currently, LED lighting and public lighting upgrades have resulted in a reduction of approximately 35% of the JPS’s total electricity consumption. The JPS has also applied to have existing high-pressure sodium (HPS) bulbs removed from the market. The JPS is also working on developing a national energy efficiency strategy, which includes LED lighting and public lighting upgrades.

Difficulties with the HPS lighting, including vandalism, have been an issue. These problems are confirmed with statistics showing that an average of 200 lamps per month were conducted by

---

# JPS due to high cost of maintenance says that it’s done. # Efforts by JPS on grid pricing is a part of Jamaica Energy Policy, to facilitate the promotion of energy savings devices & renewable energy sources. The Government of Jamaica has been working with the Ministry of Local Government and Jamaica Public Service (JPS) to develop a strategy for the implementation of the Jamaica National Energy Policy. There are no noted implementing timeframes.

## LED Lighting to Support Women and Vulnerable Groups

Provision of improved lighting services can support women’s groups, in the urban and rural areas, through the use of solar-powered lighting systems. This can help to reduce the sense of vulnerability and insecurity, and facilitate reduction of their daily safety concerns. The provision of solar-powered lighting can also improve economic opportunities for women and vulnerable groups with households that lack access to electricity. This is especially true in some cases where work hours extend beyond daylight hours; this is exemplified by the case of a woman who was able to increase her income through the use of LED lighting.

### By creating safe lighting services

Lighting services have also been shown to support the reduction of social isolation, ‘safe community’ cohesion, and decrease the number of people who are vulnerable to crime. Improved lighting services have also been shown to boost an individual’s ability to learn and increase their productivity. This is especially true for students who study at night.

### Problems and challenges

However, do exist in transitioning energy systems and improving access to electricity. These include issues such as the lack of infrastructure, limited financial resources, and the lack of political will. The Government of Jamaica has been working with the Ministry of Local Government and Jamaica Public Service (JPS) to develop a strategy for the implementation of the Jamaica National Energy Policy. There are no noted implementing timeframes.

### In such environments

The lack of proper lighting can be further exacerbated by limited infrastructure, and vulnerability, especially for women who may need to travel before dark. This is especially true in rural areas where there is little or no access to electricity. The Government of Jamaica has been working with the Ministry of Local Government and Jamaica Public Service (JPS) to develop a strategy for the implementation of the Jamaica National Energy Policy. There are no noted implementing timeframes.

### LED Lighting to Support Women and Vulnerable Groups

Provision of improved lighting services can support women’s groups, in the urban and rural areas, through the use of solar-powered lighting systems. This can help to reduce the sense of vulnerability and insecurity, and facilitate reduction of their daily safety concerns. The provision of solar-powered lighting can also improve economic opportunities for women and vulnerable groups with households that lack access to electricity. This is especially true in some cases where work hours extend beyond daylight hours; this is exemplified by the case of a woman who was able to increase her income through the use of LED lighting.

# the National Peculiarity of Jamaica: Limited and Offer Financial Services Specialising in Microfinance

Heard over fifty years considered a luxury for some, but is a part of many people's everyday lives. The ability to save money is an important aspect of financial planning that can be used with much for themselves as well as for their children and households. As the Jamaican economy continues to grow, the need for more sophisticated methods of saving and investing should be considered.

## WYF International Foundation

WYF International Foundation aims to promote sustainable development through microfinance. It is focused on providing financial services to those who are often excluded from traditional banking systems. Microfinance can help individuals and small businesses access loans, savings accounts, and other financial services at a lower cost than conventional banks. This includes:

- **Microloans**: Small loans for business purposes.
- **Savings Accounts**: Accessible with low maintenance costs as well as fees.
- **Insurance**: Protection against unexpected events.
- **Financial Education**: Empowering individuals to make informed decisions.

### Rooftop Solar Photovoltaics

The transition to a decarbonised economy can be facilitated by creating a market for renewable energy. Rooftop solar photovoltaic (PV) systems are an effective way to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and promote sustainable development. This technology is particularly useful in developing countries, where access to electricity is limited.

Women may play a crucial role in the transition to renewable energy by influencing patterns of consumption and investment. According to the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), women's exposure to indoor air pollution can be reduced through improved access to clean cooking solutions. Additionally, women are often responsible for household energy use and can therefore benefit from energy-efficient appliances and lighting.

Clean energy such as rooftop solar cannot reduce the load on the electricity grid; however, it can increase the amount of renewable energy generated in households. This can help to reduce greenhouse gas emissions while boosting the local economy by creating jobs in installation, maintenance, and sales.

### Women's Role in Sustainable Development

Women play a crucial role in sustainable development. They are often responsible for managing household resources and making decisions about energy use. According to the International Energy Agency (IEA), women's participation in the labor force is essential for achieving gender equality and promoting sustainable development.

Women's feet and socioeconomic standing emerge from having access to reliable energy. This includes living in a healthy environment expected with some of the present energy practices. There is a continuing argument for gender equity in energy use. Gender equity is the fair distribution of resources, opportunities, development, and even decision-making at the highest levels of the energy system's potential and as change agents on the front lines.

Low-income households, primarily comprised of female-headed households with young children, are most vulnerable to the effects of energy poverty. These households often live in substandard housing or informal settlements where they have no access to electricity. They have no access to clean cooking fuels, and their health is compromised by indoor air pollution from traditional biomass use. Women in these households bear the brunt of the burden of energy poverty. They are responsible for collecting fuel and water, cooking, cleaning, and caring for children. They are also responsible for raising the standard of living, and boosting productivity.

Moreover, they face additional difficulties when compared with energy-rich households. Energy-poor households have less access to education, health care, and other basic services. Energy-poor households and underclass face a need for targeted support and measures to improve their quality of life.

### Efficient Cooling and Retrofitting

Energy-related behaviours and cooling energy consumption are influenced by gender, living conditions, and income levels among others.

A gendered approach to energy efficiency is essential in China. Women have been shown to be more efficient in using air conditioners than men. This is due to their greater awareness of the need for comfort and their ability to control the thermostat. Women also tend to use less energy when cooling their homes, as they are more likely to turn off lights and fans when not in use.

### 3.3.4 Barriers

Some of the specific challenges women face in terms of affordable energy, and more specifically with free fuel as highlighted by the primary case studies in this home, include having to pay for fuel, lack of access to renewable energy sources, and the high cost of renewable energy. The lack of access to renewable energy is a barrier that affects both men and women equally.

A weak enabling policy environment for SMEs also poses a barrier to implementation. While Jamaica has made progress on renewable energy, there remain significant challenges in terms of implementation. This includes the need for more support from the government and private sector to ensure that RE and its benefits are fully realized by all citizens.

Lack of public awareness also limits the usage of SME systems. There are currently no specific programs or policies aimed at increasing women's access to renewable energy, although in other countries women have been shown to be more likely to adopt renewable energy technologies. Therefore, it should be implemented.

Another barrier that affects women is the administrative process involved when applying for the use of renewable energy sources. This includes the need to obtain permits and licenses, which can take a long time to implement, leaving an increased risk to generate energy. This process makes it difficult for women to access renewable energy technologies and services. During this period, there are also additional costs associated with obtaining these permits.

# Social

Without ensuring the provision of sustain fuel, the addition of reliable and sustainable energy services is essential to improve the living standards of the poor. The poor have limited access to modern fuels and technologies; their activity is often seen as detrimental to the environment. They need basic information about clean energy, hygiene, and health care. Providing assistance and health care services can help them overcome these limitations. Access to modern energy services including health, housing, and education for both women and their families play a key role in improving their living standards.

As existing consumers and users of domestic energy, the CDDs, e.g., washing, household cleaning and personal care, cooking fuel, women need to be involved in energy efficient and sustainable development. Women are the main users of biomass fuels and are responsible for household energy consumption. Women may also sell their children's labour energy to earn additional income. They may also engage in charcoal production or other energy-related activities. Their involvement is essential to ensure that they have access to clean, affordable, and reliable energy services.

The poor need to be empowered with the knowledge of how to use modern fuels and technologies effectively. Due to the lower disposable income compared to their richer counterparts, they often rely on traditional biomass for cooking and heating. They may not have access to electricity or other modern energy services. The poor also face a lack of basic infrastructure, no access to credit facilities, and limited access to clean water and sanitation.

Renewing socio-cultural patterns is important in addressing gender inequality. In 2008, around 67% Jamaicans lived in their own homes. Housing conditions have improved over the years. The majority of households (93%) had electricity, while only 15% of households had piped water on premises. Access to clean water and sanitation is essential for improving health outcomes.

# Multi-Country Level Analysis

## Key chapter tabularity

Though projects have been made across the region in general equality, women’s empowerment and social inclusion generally, and in energy access and affordability, significant disparities remain between countries. The gender gap in energy access is most pronounced in rural areas where a lack of access to electricity bars women as well as households from improving their livelihoods.

## 4.1 Comparative Policy Environment Analysis

The desk-based research was conducted to understand the policy environment for gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) in the energy sector across six countries. The analysis was based on a general review of the major policies in each country, including the gender-responsive policies which may be due to how recently it was developed and the guidelines around GESI. The research also included an overview of gender mainstreaming into the gender policies to ensure the objectives are met.

It is important to note that Barbados’s energy policy released in 2019 failed to mention gender, women or vulnerable groups at all putting the policy on par with Belize’s and Jamaica’s lack of inclusion of GESI. The policy is meant to drive country’s energy transition until 2030.

The general analysis indicates that most countries have policies that address GESI issues in some form or another. For example, Barbados has a gender equality policy which includes a good framework for gender mainstreaming and gender-responsive budgeting. The policy also includes a good framework for gender mainstreaming and gender-responsive budgeting. The policy also includes a good framework for gender mainstreaming and gender-responsive budgeting.

A major focus of the desk-based research was looking into the policy environment for renewable energy in each country, with 12 general policies reviewed across the six countries. The analysis included an overview of the policy environment for renewable energy in each country, with 12 general policies reviewed across the six countries. The analysis included an overview of the policy environment for renewable energy in each country, with 12 general policies reviewed across the six countries.

This framework outlines a commitment to gender equality and provides guidelines to support the implementation of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) through cultural, social, economic, political and environmental commitments, including the promotion of women's rights and empowerment within the greater Caribbean community. | Unspecified |
| Energy Policy 2013 | The energy policy for CARICOM is a strategic framework that outlines the region's commitment to sustainable development and the achievement of internationally agreed development goals. It aims to promote energy security, efficiency and accessibility and to accelerate the transition towards a low-carbon economy. | Where the energy sector is concerned, it is important to ensure that policies and regulations are developed in such a way as to support gender equality and women's empowerment within the greater CARICOM region. |

Consultations were held in 2021 for a draft gender equality strategy for the CARICOM region. Stepping up an initiative to achieve gender equality, the Caribbean Community (CARICOM) Secretariat is working on a Gender Equality Strategy that will be launched in 2023. The strategy reflects the work of the CARICOM Gender Equality Unit and the Secretariat's commitment to promoting gender equality and women's empowerment with member states and international development partners. The CARICOM Gender Equality Strategy is driven by six guiding principles.

![Figure 4-3: CARICOM Gender Equality Strategy Guiding Principles and Commitments](image_url)

This work centred on a human rights approach, which recognises every person's right to freedom from discrimination. It also recognises the need for gender equality and the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women and men. The strategy is based on the principles of non-discrimination, equality, equity, justice, respect for dignity and social inclusion, including the promotion of human rights. Within this context, the strategy aims to promote gender equality as a prerequisite for development and to ensure that the rights of women and men are fully realised in all areas of life. The strategy also recognises the importance of addressing the root causes of discrimination against women and men. The implementation principles reinforce the strategy's commitment to promoting gender equality and women's empowerment.

*Source: A Framework Act for Achieving Gender Equality in the Caribbean Community, available at [link](https://www.caricom.org/jsp/servlet/com caricom portlet action?page=636_192&_mid_=478&_tid_=105)*

# CARICOM Energy Policy

The energy policy recognises the system-wide nature of renewable energy as best suited with social, political and economic developments while safeguarding human rights, values and lifestyles present in the region. It also recognises that sustainable development is a prerequisite for achieving equitable, balanced growth which are reached through sector coordination in order to achieve the objectives set out herein.

The policy further recognises the need for a shared responsibility between governments, the private sector and consumers for a sustainable energy future. The policy also recognises the importance of regional cooperation in the development of renewable energy resources and the need for a coordinated approach to the development of these resources.

The policy further recognises that the development of renewable energy resources is a key area for achieving health, social inclusion, freedom from disease, economic empowerment, good governance, social justice and equity. The policy also recognises the importance of a just energy transition and the other existing clean and sustainable economy. While there are many challenges to be overcome in order to achieve these objectives, the policy also recognises the need for a reference to science or other credible evidence, with the context of economic empowerment, social justice and equity.

The other overarching activity reviewed from a regional perspective is the CARICOM Energy Efficiency and Conservation Programme (CEECP). This programme has been in existence since 1998 and has been implemented by the CARICOM Secretariat. The CEECP aims to promote energy efficiency and conservation as a means of reducing greenhouse gas emissions, improving energy security and reducing energy costs.

### 4.2.1 Finance

Long-term funding is essential for success. Banking transactions typically require a person to allocate a fair portion of time and effort to engage these institutions. This can be challenging, especially when the project is constrained by time due to their domestic duties and responsibilities.

- The project should consider alternative sources of financing such as grants or loans from international organizations.
- The government should also consider providing long-term funding for the project.
- The project should also explore other options for financing such as crowdfunding or microfinance.
- The project should also consider working with local banks to provide financing for the project.

# Benefits of Electricity Access

| Benefit | Explanation | Evidence |
|---------|--------------|----------|
| **Health** | Improved health and sanitation (e.g., reduced incidence of disease) | Reduced incidence of diseases such as diarrhea and dysentery due to improved access to clean water and sanitation. |
| **Economic** | Increased productivity, economic growth, and job creation | Economic growth and job creation through the establishment of new businesses and industries. |
| **Social** | Improved quality of life (e.g., better education, communication) | Better education and communication due to increased access to information and technology. |
| **Environmental** | Reduced environmental degradation (e.g., less deforestation) | Less deforestation due to reduced dependence on biomass for energy. |

From the household feedback (referred to in the Table), there is a unifying theme in the use and benefits of electricity, which is the improvement in quality of life. Benefits noted include improved health, education, communication, and economic opportunities. The cost of the technologies brings the major positive factor to the people, giving them less labor-intensive tasks.

## Barriers Across the Region

From the analysis of the three countries, a pattern of challenges has emerged. Double differences between rural and urban areas show that barriers can come to recognizing household energy technologies. What is noticeable is that the barriers are not uniform across the region. The most vulnerable groups are encouraged to participate in the process by providing access to information and technology.

There is variability poor access to and availability of new age disaggregated data from the three countries. The analysis in challenges of differing energy usage among men, women, and children. The use of energy data tools is a necessary step for the design and implementation of energy services for households. The lack of financial resources is a major barrier to the adoption of renewable energy technologies. The government needs to provide incentives that encourage the use of renewable energy sources.

The government needs to be more open to the challenges faced by various groups and that it would make it difficult to see the benefits of electricity access.

Economic barriers are heavily present in all three countries as strict requirements for loans imposed by banks in the form known as the regime and women are challenged to enhance their economic status through microfinance programs. The lack of access to credit is a major obstacle that divides labour or burdens imposed by unpaid care work. In Bolivia, indigenous people exhibit strong community ties and collective action to address these challenges. They have established cooperatives and work with the greatest effectiveness toward motion and accessibility energy solutions. Not only do they face economic barriers but also social ones, as they are challenged by time and distance imposes on them.

Society can learn from the three countries that work in the energy sector is skewed more towards men. The lack of women's participation in decision-making processes is a major obstacle to their advancement. Women are often excluded from the benefits of renewable energy projects and are not included in the planning process. This exclusionary approach is a reflection of the patriarchal society where women are seen as secondary citizens. It is also a reflection of the fact that women are not given equal opportunities to participate in the labour force, primarily unpaid care work.

# Key Conclusions

## Key Channel Takeaway

While the desired end state for an IUI project is clearly powering the longest touches and three most relevant environments of varying messages to support the impact, at present, there are some challenges that need to be addressed. The current process is not as efficient or effective as it could be. An IUI project is highly dependent on a new segment being set up correctly and efficiently. This is a complex process that requires a lot of time and effort from the team. In addition, the current process is not well understood by the team and requires a lot of manual intervention.

Based on the engagement with stakeholders and the research, there is both appetite and capability to improve the IUI project. The key challenge is how to generate multiple co-benefits. From available data and market comparative analysis, it is clear that there are significant opportunities for improvement. There is also an opportunity to generate income generation that may arise. As a result, the clients have change ambitions around derived benefits from the IUI project. This includes the need for a more integrated approach to the management of the IUI project. For example, the integration of households and businesses for technology adoption co-benefit.

Despite the challenges, many partners across the three flows (traders) are enthusiastic about the potential of the IUI project. There is also an appetite for the project to be implemented by other business units within the company. This is a positive sign that the project has the potential to be successful in the long run. The key challenge will be how to ensure that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently.

For the IUI project to be successful, it needs to be implemented and integrated into the existing processes of the project. The goal is to create a just transition project that can be implemented by the development and execution of the sector and its readiness to benefit from the IUI project. This will require a significant amount of effort and resources to achieve this goal. In addition, it will also require active and responsive policy action to ensure that the project meets its objectives.

For beliefs with large rural populations and significant indigenous sub-populations, customer outreach is critical for success. This includes engaging with local communities from the start of the project. It is important to note that the IUI project has a lot of potential to be successful in these areas.

Having a lack of order management capacity brings the government authorities who are responsible for the implementation of the IUI project into question. There is an urgent need to address this issue and ensure that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently. This will require a significant amount of effort and resources from the start of the project, as well as the first year of implementation.

# Recommendations

## Key Outputs and Takeaways

Ensure the program can fully be goal of supporting a just transition will require the execution of several key outputs and takeaways. This includes assessing existing opportunities and gaps that were found in the gender assessment, conducting gender-responsive programming, and ensuring that these are integrated into the program cycle. Additionally, it is important to identify and address any barriers that may limit women's participation in the program. This can be achieved through a variety of means such as providing training on gender equality and human rights, creating safe spaces for women, and promoting women's leadership.

Emerging from the gender assessment, some of the recommendations to be explored in this section include the following: 1) strengthening the leadership and decision-making capacities of women; 2) enhancing their participation in governance structures at all levels (local, community, group, household, and individual); and 3) ensuring that the program is responsive to the needs of vulnerable groups. The recommendations are divided into two categories: 1) general recommendations that speak to the overall program design and implementation; and 2) specific recommendations that focus on particular aspects of the program.

### 6.1.1 General Recommendations

These recommendations seek to improve programme actions and address gender equality issues in the program:

1. Partnering guidelines aimed at serving as a framework to be used by programme action teams (PATs), implementing partners, and other stakeholders to guide their work and ensure accountability with NOOs.
2. Partnering bullying exercises aimed at building strong working relationships between program action teams and implementing partners to enhance knowledge and experience sharing.
3. Fostering collaboration between the three clusters to enhance knowledge and experience sharing among them.
4. Execution of an approach gender capacity assessment of key government stakeholders and officials involved in the programme. The development of a gender resolution and action plan based on the results of the assessment.
5. More in-depth analysis of the financial tools being offered by the bank, credit unions, etc., to ensure that they are being used by the women as part of the program and competitive.


---
**Page 218**
---

8. A review of the programme’s logic framework and the design of gender targets of the prog

9. Development of training programmes that could help persons to seek employment in the energy sector while supporting the introduction and expansion of technologies.

10. Development of a gender-responsive procurement policy that ensures equal access for women-owned businesses as a general enterprise support term (ID required, larger projects, lower cost).

11. Development of business advisory services for female household with children, targeted closely to their needs and also to other vulnerable groups such as youth.

12. Consideration will need to be given to ensuring a migrating indirectly in terms of employment opportunities and the creation of new jobs and the retention of existing ones.

13. Assess lessons learned from existing and past train and grant programmes that target or support women-owned businesses, including the types of training and grants provided, implementation and also shape funding options and how these can be accessed by such groups.

### 6.1.2 Strategic environment recommendations

Given the outlined strategic nature of this programme and the many potential actors that could be involved throughout the its activities, there is a need to create a framework for gender-responsive actions that will ensure effective implementation and sustainability of the programme.

1. Development of a code of conduct for all programme actors to follow when engaging the public.
2. Development of GBV checklist to reduce the potential of GBV occurring within the project due to the increased bottom-up approach, and to ensure that this action is put into practice.
3. Development a communication strategy that aims to provide the public with timely updates on the progress of the programme and how it is being implemented in their communities.
4. Development of a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) for the programme that is easily accessible and used by the public, including women and youth.
5. Development of a monitoring and evaluation framework to ensure that the programme implementation includes and gender-sensitive communications.
6. The hosting of a feedback mechanism or anonymous suggestions to engage specific vulnerable groups in the programme.

# Gender Action Plan

## Key checklist tasks

Ensure the programme can fulfil its goal of supporting a net transition will require the execution of several key actions to ensure that gender equality is achieved and sustained. This includes assessing existing operations and plans that are found within the gender assessment. Overall, gender mainstreaming should be integrated into all aspects of the programme cycle. This includes planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. It also involves ensuring that the programme is designed in a way that supports the empowerment of women and girls, as well as promoting gender equality principles.

In the preparation of the proposal for the "Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services" (SUS) Programme, it was essential to ensure that the programmes meet both the Green Climate Fund's (GCF's) and Caribbean Development Bank's (CDB's) requirements. This included ensuring that gender mainstreaming is integrated into all aspects of the programme cycle. It was also important to identify areas of concern and weaknesses that need to be addressed in order to achieve the objectives of Just Transition.

Gender mainstreaming is the process by which gender equality can be achieved through the integration of a gender perspective across all policies, programmes, and activities. This includes ensuring that the services and training models employed by the programme are designed to meet the needs of both men and women. It also involves ensuring that the requirements of the GCF and CDB are met.

For the GCF's updated 2023 Gender Policy, GGP teams need to ensure the integration and alignment with the policy. This includes addressing gender equality and promoting social inclusion in all aspects of the programme cycle. The policy also aims to promote gender mainstreaming in climate investments.

Applying the GCF's to the RUP Programme would involve:

- Addressing and reducing gender inequality, resulting in sustainable livelihoods.
- Ensuring that women have equal access to opportunities and resources.
- Providing training and support for women to overcome shocks and risks they may face.
- Creating an enabling environment where women can participate fully in decision-making processes.

Overall, the programme aims to ensure that gender equality is promoted and supported throughout all aspects of its operations. This includes ensuring that the services and training models employed by the programme are designed to meet the needs of both men and women. It also involves ensuring that the requirements of the GCF and CDB are met.

- Support the comprehensive and systematic collection of sex-disaggregated data and analysis to inform policy and program design and monitor progress toward projected impact.

**GCP 2018 Administrative Policy:** This policy seeks to guide the initiative's engagement with IP as a partner in the implementation and sustainment of activities where engaging IP is a feasible and desirable approach that needs to be involved. The policy is guided by the following principles:

- Respect and enhance the rights of IP, their lands, territories and resources.
- Respect the right of IP to voluntary informed consent.
- Ensure the security of IP knowledge, practices and livelihood systems.
- Enhance the capacity of IP to manage their own development.
- Facilitate access to GCP resources by IP.

In this context the UISS Programme and its GCP, the IP policy would apply to the following:

- Programme activity planning seeks free, prior and informed consent when engaging with IP throughout the programme's lifespan, as well as in the design of activities.
- The programme will be mindful and responsive to residual impacts.

The CDB's Gender Equity Policy and Operational Strategy (DEPOG 2019) outlines CDB's commitment to gender equality and social inclusion. It provides a framework for the following action plans:

- Enhancing access to infrastructure services for all.
- Economic empowerment of all.
- Enhancing women's participation in decision-making processes.
- Ensuring gender-based violence for all, and
- Enhancing the capacity of all.

The guiding principles of the DEPOG that are applicable in the UISS programme include:

- Advocacy, dialogue and partnership.
- Gender equality and social inclusion perspectives and approaches.
- Institutional strengthening in BMC.
- Community engagement with women, men, boys and girls.
- Results monitoring

### 7.1.1 Implementing Partners

The GAP will be implemented and supported by various partners. For clarity, the partnerships which will be involved in the implementation of the GAP are outlined below. In addition to each partner’s role, it is important to note clearly what they are the lead on (the activity).

- **Stakeholders with responsibilities in the GAP**
  - **Local Government**: Local Government Area (LGA) Executive Team
    - **National Executives**: The national executives unless the BMOs1.
  - **Implementing Partners**: Government agencies such as ministries of energy, utilities
  - **The Accredited Energy CSOs**: Accredited Energy CSOs
  - **Other partners**: (includes NGOs that represent marginalised groups)

### 7.1.2 Policy Environments

The GAP will be implemented through strong and accountable partnerships. This GAP highlights the policy environment for energy is relatively gender-neutral for most countries. However, there are some exceptions where policies are not gender-neutral or do not exist at all. The policy environment for women’s engagement in energy is an important area to engage with partners and stakeholders. Any policy gaps identified should be addressed through the following steps:

- **Identify**: Identifying the policy gaps
- **Engage**: Engaging with relevant stakeholders
- **Develop**: Developing a plan to address the gaps
- **Implement**: Implementing the plan

There is also the issue of gender equality which is deeply and closely woven into the policy environment. The GAP will be implemented through strong partnerships that are open, transparent, accountable, and responsive to the needs of all stakeholders.

### 7.2 Gender Action Plan

This GAP is developed with the aim to ensure that all countries and regions create the right conditions for gender equality in energy access. The actions outlined below are based on the program’s Theory of Change. The second set of activities are focused on ensuring that the implementation of the GAP is inclusive, equitable, and sustainable. This will be achieved through:

- **Capacity building**: Building capacity to ensure that all stakeholders have the skills and knowledge needed to implement the GAP.
- **Monitoring and evaluation**: Monitoring and evaluating the progress of the GAP to ensure it is on track to achieve its goals.

Noting the GCF and CDM's gender requirements stated earlier, it is crucial that much of the gender mainstreaming work needed for this project (totalling USD 1,548,000), occurs as early as possible in the project cycle. This will ensure that the project's gender equality objectives are fully integrated into the project's planning, implementation, building capacity, and systems. These activities are placed to support the project's ability to meet its gender equality objectives through the integration of gender-responsive technologies and supporting a "Just Transition" in the target countries. Understanding this, the project's activities account for USD 16,800 with activities centred on building capacity and developing gender-responsive planning and implementation. Management costs are for monitoring and developing later actions.

# Appendix A Stakeholder Engagement Plan

## Background/Introduction to Stakeholder Engagement Process

Stakeholder engagement is aimed to address issues emerging from desktop-based research. Noting that energy poverty is a complex issue with multiple dimensions (e.g., financial, social, and environmental), the main goal is to understand the lived experiences of those who are affected by it. This will help to inform the development of an inclusive energy project and barriers and constraints to accessing reliable, affordable, and sustainable energy.

This approach builds on the Baseline Assessment and addresses issues considered to be outside the General Assessment and the Beyond Action Plan previously completed in the region. It is anticipated that this process will provide a better understanding of the lived experiences of those who are affected by energy poverty. This information can help to inform the development of an inclusive energy project.

## Gender and Socially Inclusive Stakeholder Needs Being Considered

When the project was focused on technical issues and safety, it provided and social inclusion was not considered as a priority. However, with the shift in focus towards addressing energy poverty, gender and social inclusion will be more important to include. This includes considering issues such as:

- **Gender Equality:** Women are often the primary users of energy in households and may face different challenges when accessing reliable, affordable, and sustainable energy.
- **Social Inclusion:** People with disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTQ2S+ persons, Indigenous peoples, and other marginalized groups may also face barriers to accessing reliable, affordable, and sustainable energy.

Given the focus of this project, the baseline findings on gender equality are included as well. The baseline findings on social inclusion will be updated in the context of the four regions. The following groups need to be considered in our engagement:

- **Women**
  - Young people
    - Indigenous youth
    - Youth with disabilities

## Barriers and Constraints to Considering States, Reliable, Affordable Energy and Lessons Learned from Previous Engagements

Gender Mainstreaming, Knowledge, Experience, Expertise and Capacity particularly related to renewable energy projects (particularly in implementing action)

- **Women:** Women are often the primary users of energy in households and may face different challenges when accessing reliable, affordable, and sustainable energy.
- **Social Inclusion:** People with disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTQ2S+ persons, Indigenous peoples, and other marginalized groups may also face barriers to accessing reliable, affordable, and sustainable energy.

Given the focus of this project, the baseline findings on gender equality are included as well. The baseline findings on social inclusion will be updated in the context of the four regions. The following groups need to be considered in our engagement:

- **Women**
  - Young people
    - Indigenous youth
    - Youth with disabilities

## Youth Engagement

Young people have been identified as a priority group for energy poverty interventions and are a significant focus of the COVID-19 response. Youth engagement is a critical aspect of the project.


---
**Page 234**
---

# LGBTQIA+ persons in representative groups, and indigenous people

Those individuals come directly where people and fun others in terms of how they currently live their lives. They are also a part of the community that has emerged from the argument to return be usage of identification, as gender identity is not always binary.

## Stakeholder Engagement Type

Stakeholder engagement will take three forms: key informant interviews (KII), group interviews and focus groups, and discussions with organizations. The KIIs are intended primarily for government agencies and others who have a primary role in the implementation of programs and all primarily about online except in cases where it would be better suited to an in-person interview.

### Key Informant Interviews

KIIs are planned primarily for government agencies and others. These interviews are intended to provide the perspectives from these organizations with a safe space to speak about the issues that they face, as well as their successes and challenges.

### Group Interviews

Group interviews are planned for some governmental organizations and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) working in the same field. The group interview will be conducted with each other during the interview or may have contributed in a limited group setting. In-person interviews are preferred but not required.

### Focused Discussions

Focused discussions are planned for NGO stakeholders with the idea that discussions with these organizations working in a particular field would create an engaging, safe space to discuss issues and challenges. This type of engagement is intended to bring together people who may need to have a different experience with each other.

A summary of the number of planned engagements per country and type of engagement is provided below:

# Professional Consultation Schedule

Statutory consultations are scheduled from April 1st to April 31st. The plan lists vote of the public holidays of Good Friday and Easter Monday occurring on April 2nd and 5th respectively. The statutory consultation will be held in the form of a public meeting at the community centers for following on recommendation with relevant organizations within the same period as necessary.

To facilitate scheduling, consultations to facilitate agreements setting out the best leads and results of the consultation process will be held on April 1st and 30th respectively. This will be designated by the country days concerned.

A draft of the schedule to be sent out to stakeholders follows.

### Draft Email to Stakeholders

Dear Sir/Madam,

The Government Bank (GDB) in collaboration with the government and citizens of three countries has approved a proposed Green Cities Development Project. The project is designed to promote sustainable development through the use of renewable energy sources. This program aims at reducing carbon emissions, promoting green growth, and improving air quality.

The final aim of the programme focuses on the promotion of renewable energy sources such as solar power, wind power, hydroelectricity, biomass, geothermal energy, and other forms of clean energy. The project also aims to promote the use of electric vehicles (EVs) and encourage the adoption of green technologies in the country.

The program also aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) and CO2 emissions through the promotion of sustainable development practices. This will help to reduce carbon emissions, improve air quality, and promote sustainable development.

GDB has collaborated with CPCS (in collaboration with FOAAB) Inc and SAEDI Consulting to develop a program that meets international standards for sustainable development. The program aims to promote renewable energy sources and encourage the use of clean technologies in the country.

To achieve this goal, the project will focus on promoting sustainable development practices through the use of renewable energy sources and encouraging the adoption of green technologies. This will help to reduce carbon emissions, improve air quality, and promote sustainable development.

The following days are allocated to the three countries to stakeholder engagement includes:

- Barbados - "3rd, 4th"
